Riesz transforms for Dirichlet spaces tamed by distributional curvature lower bounds
Syota Esaki Zi Jian Xu, Kazuhiro Kuwae

Abstract
The notion of tamed Dirichlet space was proposed by Erbar, Rigoni, Sturm and Tamanini [ 22 ] as a Dirichlet space having a weak form of Bakry-Émery curvature lower bounds in distribution sense. After their work, Braun [ 12 ] established a vector calculus for it, in particular, the space of L 2 L^{2} -normed L ∞ L^{\infty} -module describing vector fields, 1 1 -forms, Hessian in L 2 L^{2} -sense. In this framework, we establish the Littlewood-Paley-Stein inequality for 1 1 -forms as an element of L p L^{p} -cotangent bundles and boundedness of Riesz transforms, which partially solves the problem raised by Kawabi-Miyokawa [ 35 ] .



Keywords
: Strongly local Dirichlet space, tamed space, Bakry-Émery condition, smooth measures of (extended) Kato class, smooth measures of Dynkin class,

L^{p}
-normed
L^{\infty}
-module, Hilbert module, tensor product of Hilbert module,
L^{2}
-differential forms,

L^{2}
-vector fields,
Littlewood-Paley-Stein inequality, Riesz transform, Bochner Laplacian, Hodge-Kodaira Laplacian,
measure valued Bochner-Wietzenböck formula,
intertwining property, Wiener space,
path space with Gibbs measure, RCD-space, weighted Riemannian manifold,
Riemmanian manifold with boundary, configuration space,

Mathematics Subject Classification (2020)
: Primary 31C25, 60H15, 60J60 ; Secondary 30L15, 53C21, 58J35,35K05, 42B05, 47D08

1

Statement of Main Theorem

1.1

Framework

Let
(M,\tau)
be a topological Lusin space, i.e., a continuous injective image of a Polish
space, endowed with a
\sigma
-finite Borel measure
\mathfrak{m}
on
M
with full topological support.
Let
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
be a quasi-regular symmetric strongly local Dirichlet space on
L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})

and
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the associated symmetric sub-Markovian strongly continuous semigroup on
L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
(see
[
43
, Chapter IV, Definition 3]
for the quasi-regularity and see
[
36
, Theorem 5.1(i)]
for the strong locality).
Then there exists an
\mathfrak{m}
-symmetric special standard process
{\bf X}=(\Omega,X_{t},\mathbb{P}_{x})

associated with
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
, i.e. for
f\in L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap\mathscr{B}(M)
,

P_{t}f(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}[f(X_{t})]

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e.
x\in M
(see
[
43
, Chapter IV, Section 3]
).
The
\mathfrak{m}
-symmetry of
{\bf X}
,
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}
can be extended to a strongly continuous contraction
semigroup on
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
for
p\in[1,+\infty[
. Denote by
(D(\Delta_{p}),\Delta_{p})
its generator on
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
defined by

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}D(\Delta_{p})&\displaystyle{:=\left\{f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})\mid\exists\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{P_{t}f-f}{t}\text{ in }L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})\right\}},\\
\Delta_{p}f&\displaystyle{:=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{P_{t}f-f}{t}\text{ for }f\in D(\Delta_{p})}.\end{array}\right.

An increasing sequence
\{F_{n}\}
of closed subsets is called an
\mathscr{E}
-nest
if

\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}D(\mathscr{E})_{F_{n}}
is
\mathscr{E}_{1}
-dense in
D(\mathscr{E})
, where

D(\mathscr{E})_{F_{n}}:=\{u\in D(\mathscr{E})\mid u=0\;\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.~on }M\setminus F_{n}\}
.
A subset
N
is said to be
\mathscr{E}
-exceptional
or
\mathscr{E}
-polar
if
there exists an
\mathscr{E}
-nest
\{F_{n}\}
such that
N\subset\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}(M\setminus F_{n})
.
For two subsets
A,B
of
M
, we write
A\subset B

\mathscr{E}
-q.e. if
A\setminus B
is
\mathscr{E}
-exceptional, hence we write
A=B

\mathscr{E}
-q.e. if
A\setminus B

\mathscr{E}
-q.e. and

B\setminus A

\mathscr{E}
-q.e. hold.
It is known that any
\mathscr{E}
-exceptional set is
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible and for any
\mathfrak{m}
-negligible

\mathscr{E}
-quasi-open set is
\mathscr{E}
-exceptional, in particular, for an
\mathscr{E}
-quasi continuous function
u
,
u\geq 0

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. implies
u\geq 0

\mathscr{E}
-q.e.
For a statement
P(x)
with respect to
x\in M
, we say that
P(x)
holds
\mathscr{E}
-q.e.
x\in M
(simply
P
holds
\mathscr{E}
-q.e.) if the set
\{x\in M\mid P(x)\text{ holds }\}
is
\mathscr{E}
-exceptional.
A subset
G
of
M
is called an
\mathscr{E}
-quasi-open set
if there exists an
\mathscr{E}
-nest
\{F_{n}\}
such that
G\cap F_{n}
is an open set of
F_{n}
with respect to the relative topology on

F_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A function
u
on
M
is said to be
\mathscr{E}
-quasi continuous
if there exists an
\mathscr{E}
-nest
\{F_{n}\}
such that
u|_{F_{n}}
is continuous on
F_{n}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Denote by
(\mathcal{E},D(\mathcal{E})_{e})
the extended Dirichlet space of
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
defined by

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}D(\mathcal{E})_{e}&=\{u\in L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})\mid\exists\{u_{n}\}\subset D(\mathscr{E}):\mathscr{E}\text{-Cauchy sequence}\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt\text{ such that }\lim_{n\to\infty}u_{n}=u\;\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}\},\\
\mathscr{E}(u,u)&=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{E}(u_{n},u_{n}),\end{array}\right.

(see
[
25
, p. 40]
for details on extended Dirichlet space).
Denote by
\dot{D}(\mathscr{E})_{{\rm loc}}
, the space of functions locally in
D(\mathscr{E})
in the broad sense defined
by

\displaystyle\dot{D}(\mathscr{E})_{{\rm loc}}

\displaystyle=\{u\in L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})\mid\exists\{u_{n}\}\subset D(\mathscr{E})\text{ and }\exists\{G_{n}\}:\mathscr{E}\text{-nest of }\mathscr{E}\text{-quasi-open sets}

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\text{ such that }u=u_{n}\;\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.~on }G_{n}\}.

Here an increasing sequence
\{G_{n}\}
of
\mathscr{E}
-quasi open sets is called an

\mathscr{E}
-nest
if
M=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}G_{n}

\mathscr{E}
-q.e.
For
u\in\dot{D}(\mathscr{E})_{{\rm loc}}
and an
\mathscr{E}
-nest
\{G_{n}\}

of
\mathscr{E}
-quasi-open sets satisfying
u|_{G_{n}}\in D(\mathscr{E})|_{G_{n}}
, we write
\{G_{n}\}\in\Xi(u)
. It is known that
D(\mathscr{E})\subset D(\mathscr{E})_{e}\subset\dot{D}(\mathscr{E})_{{\rm loc}}
and
each
u\in\dot{D}(\mathscr{E})_{{\rm loc}}
admits an
\mathscr{E}
-quasi continuous
\mathfrak{m}
-version (see
[
36
]
).

It is known that for
u,v\in D(\mathscr{E})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
there exists a unique signed finite Borel
measure
\mu_{\langle u,v\rangle}
on
M
such that

\displaystyle 2\int_{M}\tilde{f}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\langle u,v\rangle}=\mathscr{E}(uf,v)+\mathscr{E}(vf,u)-\mathscr{E}(uv,f)\quad\text{ for }\quad u,v\in D(\mathscr{E})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m}).

Here
\tilde{f}
denotes the
\mathscr{E}
-quasi-continuous
\mathfrak{m}
-version of
f
(see
[
25
, Theorem 2.1.3]
,
[
43
, Chapter IV, Proposition 3.3(ii)]
).
We set
\mu_{\langle f\rangle}:=\mu_{\langle f,f\rangle}
for
f\in D(\mathscr{E})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
.
Moreover,
for
f,g\in D(\mathscr{E})
, there exists a signed finite measure
\mu_{\langle f,g\rangle}
on
M
such that

\mathscr{E}(f,g)=\mu_{\langle f,g\rangle}(M)
, hence
\mathscr{E}(f,f)=\mu_{\langle f\rangle}(M)
.
We assume
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
admits a carré-du-champ
\Gamma
, i.e.

\mu_{\langle f\rangle}\ll\mathfrak{m}
for all
f\in D(\mathscr{E})
and set
\Gamma(f):=\mathrm{d}\mu_{\langle f\rangle}/\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}
.
Then
\mu_{\langle f,g\rangle}\ll\mathfrak{m}
for all
f,g\in D(\mathscr{E})
and
\Gamma(f,g):=\mathrm{d}\mu_{\langle f,g\rangle}/{\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}}\in L^{1}(M;\mathfrak{m})
is expressed by
\Gamma(f,g)=\frac{1}{4}(\Gamma(f+g)-\Gamma(f-g))
for
f,g\in D(\mathscr{E})
.

We can extend the singed smooth
\mu_{\langle f,g\rangle}
or carré-du-champ
\Gamma(f,g)
from
f,g\in D(\mathscr{E})
to

f,g\in\dot{D}(\mathscr{E})_{{\rm loc}}
by polarization (see
[
36
, Lemmas 5.2 and 5.3]
). In this case,
\mu_{\langle f,g\rangle}
(resp.
\Gamma(f,g)
)
is no longer a finite signed measure (resp. an
\mathfrak{m}
-integrable function). For
u\in\dot{D}(\mathscr{E})_{{\rm loc}}
and
an
\mathscr{E}
-nest
\{G_{n}\}

of
\mathscr{E}
-quasi-open sets satisfying
u|_{G_{n}}\in D(\mathscr{E})|_{G_{n}}
, then we can define
\mathscr{E}(u,v)

for
v\in\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}D(\mathscr{E})_{G_{n}}
by

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}(u,v)=\mu_{\langle u,v\rangle}(M).

Here
D(\mathscr{E})_{G_{n}}:=\{u\in D(\mathscr{E})\mid\tilde{u}=0\;\mathscr{E}\text{-q.e.~on }G_{n}^{c}\}
.

Fix
q\in\{1,2\}
. Let
\kappa
be a signed smooth measure with its Jordan-Hahn decomposition
\kappa=\kappa^{+}-\kappa^{-}
.
We assume that
\kappa^{+}
is a smooth measure of Dynkin class (
\kappa^{+}\in S_{D}({\bf X})
in short)
and
2\kappa^{-}
is a smooth measure of extended Kato class (
2\kappa^{-}\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
in short).
More precisely,
\nu\in S_{D}({\bf X})
(resp.
\nu\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
) if and only if
\nu\in S({\bf X})
and

\mathfrak{m}\text{-}\sup_{x\in M}\mathbb{E}_{x}[A_{t}^{\nu}]<\infty
for any/some
t>0

(resp.
\lim_{t\to 0}\mathfrak{m}\text{-}\sup_{x\in M}\mathbb{E}_{x}[A_{t}^{\nu}]<1
) (see
[
2
]
).
Here
S({\bf X})
denotes the family of smooth measures with respect to
{\bf X}
(see
[
43
, Chapter VI, Definition 2.3]
,
[
25
, p. 83]
for the definition of smooth measures) and
\mathfrak{m}
-
\sup_{x\in M}f(x)
denotes the
\mathfrak{m}
-essentially supremum for a function
f
on
M
.
For
\nu\in S({\bf X})
and set
U_{\alpha}\nu(x):=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha t}\mathrm{d}A_{t}^{\nu}\right]
with its
\mathfrak{m}
-essentially supremum
\|U_{\alpha}\nu\|_{\infty}:=\mathfrak{m}
-
\sup_{x\in M}U_{\alpha}\nu(x)
,
\|U_{\alpha}\nu\|_{\infty}<\infty
for some/any
\alpha>0

(resp.
\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\|U_{\alpha}\nu\|_{\infty}=0
,
\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}\|U_{\alpha}\nu\|_{\infty}<1
)
if and only if
\nu\in S_{D}({\bf X})
(resp.
\nu\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
,
\nu\in S_{K}({\bf X})
).
For
\nu\in S_{D}({\bf X})
, the following inequality holds

\displaystyle\int_{M}\tilde{f}^{2}\mathrm{d}\nu\leq\|U_{\alpha}\nu\|_{\infty}\mathscr{E}_{\alpha}(f,f),\quad f\in D(\mathscr{E})

(1.1)

which is called the Stollmann-Voigt’s inequality. Here
\mathscr{E}_{\alpha}(f,f):=\mathscr{E}_{\alpha}(f,f)+\alpha\|f\|_{L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})}^{2}
.
Then, for
q\in\{1,2\}
, the quadratic form

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}^{q\kappa}(f):=\mathscr{E}(f)+\langle 2\kappa,\tilde{f}^{2}\rangle

with finiteness domain
D(\mathscr{E}^{q\kappa})=D(\mathscr{E})
is closed, lower semi-bounded, moreover,
there exists
\alpha_{0}>0
and
C>0
such that

\displaystyle C^{-1}\mathscr{E}_{1}(f,f)\leq\mathscr{E}^{q\kappa}_{\alpha_{0}}(f,f)\leq C\mathscr{E}_{1}(f,f)\quad\text{ for all }\quad f\in D(\mathscr{E}^{q\kappa})=D(\mathscr{E})

(1.2)

by (
1.1
) (see
[
14
, (3.8)]
and
[
15
, Assumption of Theorem 1.1]
).
The Feynman-Kac semigroup
(p_{t}^{q\kappa})_{t\geq 0}
defined by

\displaystyle p_{t}^{q\kappa}f(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}[e^{-qA_{t}^{\kappa}}f(X_{t})],\quad f\in\mathscr{B}_{b}(M)

is
\mathfrak{m}
-symmetric, i.e.

\displaystyle\int_{M}p_{t}^{q\kappa}f(x)g(x)\mathfrak{m}(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{M}g(x)p_{t}^{q\kappa}g(x)\mathfrak{m}(\mathrm{d}x)\quad\text{ for all }\quad f,g\in\mathscr{B}_{+}(M)

and coincides with the strongly continuous semigroup
(P_{t}^{q\kappa})_{t\geq 0}
on
L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
associated with

(\mathscr{E}^{q\kappa},D(\mathscr{E}^{q\kappa}))
(see
[
15
, Theorem 1.1]
).
Here
A_{t}^{q\kappa}
is a continuous additive functional (CAF in short) associated with the signed smooth measure
q\kappa
under Revuz correspondence.
Under
\kappa^{-}\in S_{K}({\bf X})
and
p\in[1,+\infty]
,

(p_{t}^{\kappa})_{t\geq 0}
can be extended
to be a bounded operator on
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
denoted by
P_{t}^{\kappa}
such that
there exist finite constants
C=C(\kappa)>0,C_{\kappa}\geq 0
depending only on
\kappa^{-}
such that
for every
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\|P_{t}^{\kappa}\|_{p,p}\leq Ce^{C_{\kappa}t}.

(1.3)

Here
C=1
under
\kappa^{-}=0
.

C_{\kappa}\geq 0

can be taken to be
0
under
\kappa^{-}=0
.

Let
\Delta^{q\kappa}
be the
L^{2}
-generator associated with
(\mathscr{E}^{q\kappa},D(\mathscr{E}))

defined by

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}D(\Delta^{q\kappa})&:=\{u\in D(\mathscr{E})\mid\text{there exists }w\in L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})\text{ such that }\\
&\hskip 85.35826pt\mathscr{E}^{q\kappa}(u,v)=-\int_{M}wv\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}\quad\text{ for any }\quad v\in D(\mathscr{E})\},\\
\Delta^{q\kappa}u&:=w\quad\text{ for }w\in L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})\quad\text{specified as above,}\end{array}\right.

called the
Schrödinger operator
with potential
q\kappa
.
Formally,
\Delta^{q\kappa}
can be understood as “
\Delta^{q\kappa}=\Delta-q\kappa
”,
where
\Delta
is the
L^{2}
-generator associated with
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
.

Definition 1.1

(
q
-Bakry-Émery condition)
.

Suppose that
q\in\{1,2\}
,
\kappa^{+}\in S_{D}({\bf X})
,
2\kappa^{-}\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
and
N\in[1,+\infty]
. We say that
(M,\mathscr{E},\mathfrak{m})
or simply
M
satisfies the
q
-Bakry-Émery condition, briefly

{\sf BE}_{q}(\kappa,N)
, if for every
f\in D(\Delta)
with
\Delta f\in D(\mathscr{E})

and every nonnegative
\phi\in D(\Delta^{q\kappa})
with

\Delta^{q\kappa}\phi\in L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
(we further impose
\phi\in L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
for
q=2
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\int_{M}\Delta^{q\kappa}\phi\Gamma(f)^{\frac{q}{2}}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}-\int_{M}\phi\Gamma(f)^{\frac{q-2}{2}}\Gamma(f,\Delta f)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}\geq\frac{1}{N}\int_{M}\phi\Gamma(f)^{\frac{q-2}{2}}(\Delta f)^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}.

The latter term is understood as
0
if
N=\infty
.

Assumption 1
.

We assume that
M
satisfies
{\sf BE}_{2}(\kappa,N)
condition for a given signed smooth measure

\kappa
with
\kappa^{+}\in S_{D}({\bf X})
and
2\kappa^{-}\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
and
N\in[1,\infty]
.

Under Assumption
1
, we say that
(M,\mathscr{E},\mathfrak{m})
or simply
M
is
tamed
.
In fact, under
\kappa^{+}\in S_{D}({\bf X})
and
2\kappa^{-}\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
, the condition
{\sf BE}_{2}(\kappa,\infty)
is
equivalent

to
{\sf BE}_{1}(\kappa,\infty)
(see Lemma
3.2
below), in particular, the heat flow
(P_{t})_{t\geq 0}

satisfies

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Gamma(P_{t}f)}\leq P_{t}^{\kappa}\sqrt{\Gamma(f)}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.~for any }f\in D(\mathscr{E})\quad\text{ and }\quad t\geq 0

(1.7)

(see
[
22
, Definition 3.3 and Theorem 3.4]
).
The inequality (
1.7
) plays a crucial role in our paper.
Note that our condition
\kappa^{+}\in S_{D}({\bf X})
,
\kappa^{-}\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
(resp.
\kappa^{+}\in S_{D}({\bf X})
,
2\kappa^{-}\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
) is stronger than the
1
-moderate (resp.
2
-moderate) condition treated in
[
22
]
for the definition of tamed space.
The
\mathfrak{m}
-symmetric Markov process
{\bf X}
treated in our paper may not be conservative in general. Under Assumption
1
,
a sufficient condition (
intrinsic stochastic completeness
called in
[
22
]
)
for conservativeness of
{\bf X}
is discussed in
[
22
, Section 3.3]
, in
particular, under
1\in D(\mathscr{E})
and Assumption
1
, we have the conservativeness of
{\bf X}
.

Our main theorem under Assumption
1
is the following:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
p\in]1,+\infty[
and
\alpha>C_{\kappa}
. For
f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, we define the Riesz operator

R_{\alpha}(\Delta)
by

\displaystyle R_{\alpha}(\Delta)f:=\Gamma((\alpha-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}f)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Suppose
\kappa^{-}\in S_{K}({\bf X})
and
p\in[2,+\infty[
, or
\kappa_{\ll}^{-}=-R\mathfrak{m}
with
R\geq 0
,
\kappa_{\perp}^{-}=0
and
p\in]1,2]
.
Then,

R_{\alpha}(\Delta)
can be extended to a (non-linear) bounded operator on
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
.
The operator norm
\|R_{\alpha}(\Delta)\|_{p,p}
depends only on
\kappa,p
and
C_{\kappa}
.

Fix
p\in]1,\infty[
. We
define the
(1,p)
-Sobolev space
(H^{1,p}(M),\|\cdot\|_{H^{1,p}})
as follows:

Definition 1.3
.

Let
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
be a quasi-regular strongly local Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
admitting
carré du champ
\Gamma
.
Set
\mathscr{D}_{1,p}:=\{u\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap D(\mathscr{E})\mid\Gamma(u)^{\frac{1}{2}}\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})\}
.
For
u\in\mathscr{D}_{1,p}
, we define
\|u\|_{H^{1,p}}^{p}:=\|u\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}^{p}+\|\Gamma(u)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}^{p}
. Then
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1,p}}
forms a norm on
\mathscr{D}_{1,p}
.
We now define the Sobolev space
H^{1,p}(M)
as the
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1,p}}
-completion of
\mathscr{D}_{1,p}
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}H^{1,p}(M)&:=\overline{\mathscr{D}_{1,p}}^{\|\cdot\|_{H^{1,p}}},\\
\|u\|_{H^{1,p}}&:=\left(\|u\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}^{p}+\|\Gamma(u)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\quad\text{ for }\quad u\in H^{1,p}(M).\end{array}\right.

More precisely, for
u\in H^{1,p}(M)
, we can extend the carré du champ
\Gamma
to
u
with
\Gamma(u)^{\frac{1}{2}}\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
.
We set
D(\mathscr{E}^{\,p}):=H^{1,p}(M)
and
\mathscr{E}^{\,p}(u):=\|\Gamma(u)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}^{p}
for
u\in D(\mathscr{E}^{p})
, which is called the
p
-energy of
u\in H^{1,p}(M)
.

It is proved in
[
38
, Lemma 3.1]
that
(H^{1,p}(M),\|\cdot\|_{H^{1,p}})
is a uniformly convex Banach space and consequently it is reflexive.

The following theorems are essentially shown in the framework of weighted Riemannian manifolds
(M,g,e^{-\phi}v_{g})
(without boundary) with Witten Laplacian
L:=\Delta-\langle\nabla\phi,\cdot\rangle
. For the completeness, we restate them in our framework under Assumption
1
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Fix
\alpha>C_{\kappa}
.
The following statements are equivalent, and one of them (hence all) holds under

\kappa^{-}\in S_{K}({\bf X})
and
p\in[2,+\infty[
, or under
\kappa_{\ll}^{-}=-R\mathfrak{m}
with
R\geq 0
,
\kappa_{\perp}^{-}=0
and
p\in]1,2]
.

(1)

The Riesz transform
R_{\alpha}(\Delta)
is bounded in
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
for all
p\in]1,+\infty[
. That is, for every
p\in]1,+\infty[
, there exists a constant
\|R_{\alpha}(\Delta)\|_{p,p}\in]0,+\infty[
such that

\displaystyle\|R_{\alpha}(\Delta)f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\leq\|R_{\alpha}(\Delta)\|_{p,p}\|f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})},\quad f\in{\rm Test}(M)_{fs},

where
{\rm Test}(M)_{fs}
is the family of test functions with finite supports
(
see Definition
3.3
below
)
.

(2)

The Sobolev norms
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1,p}}
,

\|\cdot\|_{1,p}
and
\||\cdot|\|_{1,p}
are equivalent, where

\displaystyle\|f\|_{1,p}:

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\alpha}\|f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}+\|\Gamma(f)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})},

\displaystyle\||f|\|_{1,p}:

\displaystyle=\|(\alpha-\Delta_{p})^{\frac{1}{2}}f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}

for
f\in H^{1,p}(M)
.

(3)

The Sobolev space
H^{1,p}(M)
coincides with the Sobolev space
W^{1,p}(M)
defined by
W^{1,p}(M):=V_{\alpha}(L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m}))
. Here
V_{\alpha}
is the bounded linear operator on
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
defined by
V_{\alpha}f:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha t}t^{-\frac{1}{2}}P_{t}f\mathrm{d}t=(\alpha-\Delta_{p})^{-\frac{1}{2}}f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
satisfying
\sqrt{\alpha}\|V_{\alpha}f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}
.

(4)

The
(1,p)
-capacities
c_{1,p}
and
C_{1,p}
are equivalent, where

\displaystyle c_{1,p}(O):

\displaystyle=\inf\{\|f\|_{1,p}^{p}\mid f\in H^{1,p}(M),f\geq 0\;\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.~on }M,f\geq 1\;\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.~on }O\},

\displaystyle C_{1,p}(O):

\displaystyle=\inf\{\|f\|_{1,p}^{p}\mid f\in W^{1,p}(M),f\geq 0\;\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.~on }M,f\geq 1\;\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.~on }O\},

for all open sets
O\subset X
, and for all
A\subset X
,

\displaystyle c_{1,p}(A):

\displaystyle=\inf\{c_{1,p}(O)\mid f\in H^{1,p}(M),A\subset O\subset X,O\text{ is open}\},

\displaystyle C_{1,p}(A):

\displaystyle=\inf\{C_{1,p}(O)\mid f\in W^{1,p}(M),A\subset O\subset X,O\text{ is open}\}.

Proof.

The proof is quite similar with the proof of
[
40
, Theorem 3.1]
. We omit it.
∎

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
p\in]1,+\infty[
. Suppose that the Riesz transform
R_{\alpha}(\Delta)
is bounded in
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
for some
\alpha\geq 0
.
Then
R_{\beta}(\Delta)
is bounded in
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
for all
\beta>\alpha\wedge 0
.
Moreover, there exists a constant
A_{p}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|R_{\beta}(\Delta)\|_{p,p}\leq A_{p}(\sqrt{\alpha\beta^{-1}}\lor 1)\|R_{\alpha}(\Delta)\|_{p,p}.

Proof.

The proof is quite similar with the proof of
[
40
, Theorem 3.2]
. We omit it.
∎

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
p\in]1,+\infty[
. Suppose that the Riesz transform
R_{\alpha}(\Delta):=\Gamma(\nabla(\alpha-\Delta_{p})f)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is bounded in
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
for some
\alpha>0
and the bottom of spectrum of
-\Delta
is strictly positive, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}(-\Delta):=\inf_{f\in D(\mathscr{E})\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\mathscr{E}(f,f)}{\;\quad\|f\|_{L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})}^{2}}.

Then the Riesz
R_{0}(\Delta):=\Gamma(\nabla(-\Delta_{p})f)^{\frac{1}{2}}
is bounded in
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
.

Proof.

The proof is quite similar with the proof of
[
40
, Theorem 3.3]
. We omit it.
∎

Under Assumption
1
, we have also the following:

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
p\in]1,+\infty[
and
\alpha>C_{\kappa}
. Suppose
\kappa^{-}\in S_{K}({\bf X})
under
p\in[2,+\infty[
, or
\kappa_{\ll}=-R\mathfrak{m}
,
\kappa_{\perp}=0
under
p\in]1,2]
. Then we have the following:

(1)

There exists
A_{p}>0
such that for any
t>0
and
f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(P_{t}f)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\leq A_{p}\|R_{\alpha}(\Delta)\|_{p,p}(\alpha^{1/2}+t^{-1/2})\|f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}.

(1.8)

(2)

There exists
A_{p}>0
such that for any
{\varepsilon}\in]0,1[
,
t>0
and
f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(P_{t}f)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\leq A_{p}\frac{\|R_{\alpha}(\Delta)\|_{p,p}}{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}\cdot\frac{e^{\alpha{\varepsilon}t}}{\sqrt{t}}\|f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}.

(1.9)

(3)

If the heat semigroup
P_{t}
is hypercontractive, i.e.,
\|P_{t}\|_{p,q(t)}=m_{p,q}(t)
, then
there exists
C_{p}>0
such that for any
{\varepsilon}\in]0,1[
.
t>0
and
f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(P_{t}f)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\leq C_{p}\frac{\|R_{\alpha}(\Delta)\|_{p,p}}{\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}}\cdot\frac{e^{\alpha{\varepsilon}t}m_{p,q}((1-{\varepsilon})t)}{\sqrt{t}}\|f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}.

(1.10)

(4)

There exists
A_{p}>0
such that, for any
{\varepsilon}\in]0,1[
,
t>0
and
f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle\|\Gamma(Q_{t}^{(\alpha)}f)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\leq C_{p}\frac{\|R_{\alpha}(\Delta)\|_{p,p}}{{\varepsilon}}\cdot\frac{e^{-\sqrt{\alpha}{\varepsilon}t}}{t}\|f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})},

(1.11)

where
Q_{t}^{(\alpha)}f:=e^{-t\sqrt{\alpha-\Delta}}f
is the Cauchy semigroup.

Proof.

The proof is quite similar with the proof of
[
40
, Theorem 3.4]
. We omit it.
∎

2

Smooth measures of extended Kato class

In this section, we summarize results on smooth measures of extended Kato class.
Denote by
S({\bf X})
the family of (non-negative) smooth measures on
(M,\mathscr{B}(M))
(see
[
25
, p. 83]
for the precise
definition of smooth measures). Then there exists a PCAF
A^{\nu}
admitting exceptional set satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{h\mathfrak{m}}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha t}f(X_{t})\mathrm{d}A_{t}^{\nu}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{f\nu}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha t}f(X_{t})\mathrm{d}t\right],\quad f,h\in\mathscr{B}_{+}(M)

(see
[
25
, (5.14)]
).
A smooth measure
\nu
is said to be of
Dynkin class
(resp.
Kato class
) if
\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t}^{\nu}]\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}<\infty
for some/all
t>0
(resp.
\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t}^{\nu}]\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}=0
).
A smooth measure
\nu
is said to be of
extended Kato class
if
\lim_{t\to 0}\|\mathbb{E}_{x}[A_{t}^{\nu}]\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}<1
. Denote by
S_{D}({\bf X})
(resp.
S_{K}({\bf X})
,
S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
) the family of
(non-negative) smooth measures of Dynkin class (resp. Kato class, extended Kato class). For
\nu\in S_{D}({\bf X})
,

x\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{x}[A_{t}^{\nu}]
is
\mathscr{E}
-quasi-continuous, hence
\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t}^{\nu}]\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}
actually coincides with a quasi-essentially supremum:

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t}^{\nu}]\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}=q\text{-}\sup_{x\in M}\mathbb{E}_{x}[A_{t}^{\nu}]:=\inf_{N:\mathscr{E}\text{-exceptional}}\sup_{x\in N^{c}}\mathbb{E}_{x}[A_{t}^{\nu}].

For
\nu\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
, there exist positive constants
C=C(\nu)
and
C_{\nu}>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[e^{A_{t}^{\nu}}]\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\leq Ce^{C_{\nu}t}.

(2.1)

Indeed, we can choose
C(\nu):=(1-\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t_{0}}^{\nu}]\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})})^{-1}
and
C_{\nu}:=\frac{1}{t_{0}}\log C(\nu)
.
for some sufficiently small
t_{0}\in]0,1[
satisfying
\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t_{0}}^{\nu}]\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}<1
(see
[
54
, Proof of Theorem 2.2]
). Denote by
P_{t}^{-\nu}f(x)
the Feynman-Kac semigroup defined by

P_{t}^{-\nu}f(x):=\mathbb{E}_{x}[e^{A_{t}^{\nu}}f(X_{t})]
,
f\in\mathscr{B}_{+}(M)
. (
2.1
) is nothing but

\|P_{t}^{-\nu}\|_{\infty,\infty}\leq Ce^{C_{\nu}t}
, moreover,
\|P_{t}^{-\nu}\|_{p,p}\leq Ce^{C_{\nu}t}
for
p\in[2,+\infty]
.
Here
\|P_{t}^{-\nu}\|_{p,p}:=\sup\{\|P_{t}^{-\nu}f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\mid f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m}),\|f\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}=1\}
stands for the operator norm from

L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
to
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
.
For each
\beta\geq 1
satisfying
\beta\nu\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
, we can choose

\displaystyle C_{\beta\nu}\in]0,C_{\nu}]\quad\text{ and }\quad C(\nu)=C(\beta\nu).

(2.2)

Indeed
C_{\nu}
and
C(\nu)
can be given by

\displaystyle C_{\nu}=\frac{1}{t_{0}}\log\left(1-\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t_{0}}^{\nu}]\|_{\infty}\right)^{-1},\quad C(\nu)=\left(1-\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t_{0}}^{\nu}]\|_{\infty}\right)^{-1}

for some small
t_{0}\in]0,1]
satisfying
\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t_{0}}^{\nu}]\|_{\infty}<1
(see
[
54
, Proof of Theorem 2.2]
).
If we choose
s_{0}\in]0,t_{0}]

satisfying
\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{s_{0}}^{\nu}]\|_{\infty}=\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t_{0}}^{\beta\nu}]\|_{\infty}<1
, then we see
C_{\beta\nu}\leq C_{\nu}
and
C(\nu)=C(\beta\nu)
for such selection of
s_{0}
(resp.
t_{0}
) to
C_{\nu}
(resp.
C_{\beta\kappa}
).

When
\nu=-R\mathfrak{m}
for a constant
R\in\mathbb{R}
,
C_{\nu}
(resp.
C(\nu)
) can be taken to be
R\lor 0
(resp.
1
).

The following lemma might be known for experts:

Lemma 2.1
.

For
\nu\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
and
\alpha>C_{\nu}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha t}\mathrm{d}A_{t}^{\nu}\right]\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}<1.

Proof.

Take any sufficiently small
t_{0}\in]0,T[
.
By use of Markov property,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha t}\mathrm{d}A_{t}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\int_{nt_{0}}^{(n+1)t_{0}}e^{-\alpha t}\mathrm{d}A_{t}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\int_{0}^{t_{0}}e^{-\alpha(nt_{0}+t)}\mathrm{d}A_{t}^{\nu}\circ\theta_{nt_{0}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha nt_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\mathbb{E}_{X_{nt_{0}}}\left[\int_{0}^{t_{0}}e^{-\alpha t}\mathrm{d}A_{t}^{\nu}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha nt_{0}}q\text{-}\sup_{x\in M}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[A_{t_{0}}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-e^{-\alpha t_{0}}}\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}[A_{t_{0}}^{\nu}]\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-e^{-C_{\nu}(t_{0})t_{0}}}{1-e^{-\alpha t_{0}}}.

Then

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{E}_{\cdot}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha t}\mathrm{d}A_{t}^{\nu}\right]\right\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\leq\frac{1-e^{-C_{\nu}(t_{0})t_{0}}}{1-e^{-\alpha t_{0}}}<1

under
\alpha>C_{\nu}(t_{0})
.
∎

Corollary 2.2
.

For
\nu\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
and
\alpha>C_{\nu}
,

\displaystyle(1-\|U_{\alpha}\nu\|_{\infty})\mathscr{E}(f,f)\leq\mathscr{E}^{-\nu}_{\alpha\|U_{\alpha}\nu\|_{\infty}}(f,f)\leq\mathscr{E}_{\alpha\|U_{\alpha}\nu\|_{\infty}}(f,f)\quad\text{ for }\quad f\in D(\mathscr{E}),

(2.3)

where
U_{\alpha}\nu(x):=\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha t}\mathrm{d}A_{t}^{\nu}\right]
. In particular,
under
\kappa^{+}\in S_{D}({\bf X})
and
\kappa^{-}\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
, for
\alpha>C_{\kappa}
, we have

\displaystyle(1-\|U_{\alpha}\kappa^{-}\|_{\infty})\mathscr{E}(f,f)\leq\mathscr{E}^{\kappa}_{\alpha\|U_{\alpha}\kappa^{-}\|_{\infty}}(f,f)\leq C\mathscr{E}_{1}(f,f)\quad\text{ for }\quad f\in D(\mathscr{E}),

(2.4)

where
C:=\max\left\{(1+\|U_{\alpha}\kappa^{+}\|_{\infty}),\alpha\|U_{\alpha}|\kappa|\|_{\infty}\right\}
.

3

Test functions

The following lemmas hold.

Lemma 3.1

(
[
22
, Proposition 6.7]
)
.

Under Assumption
1
,
{\sf BE}_{2}(-\kappa^{-},+\infty)
holds. Moreover, for every
f\in L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
and
t>0
, it holds

\displaystyle\Gamma(P_{t}f)\leq\frac{1}{2t}\|P_{t}^{-\kappa^{-}}\|_{\infty,\infty}\cdot\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}^{2}.

(3.1)

In particular, for
f\in L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, then
P_{t}f\in{\rm Test}(M)
.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
22
, Theorems 3.4 and 3.6, Proposition 3.7 and Theorem 6.10]
)
.

Under
\kappa^{+}\in S_{D}({\bf X})
and
2\kappa^{-}\in S_{E\!K}({\bf X})
,
the condition
{\sf BE}_{2}(\kappa,\infty)
is equivalent
to
{\sf BE}_{1}(\kappa,\infty)
. In particular, we have (
1.7
).

Under Assumption
1
, we now introduce
{\rm Test}(M)
the set of test functions:

Definition 3.3
.

Let
(M,\mathscr{E},\mathfrak{m})
be a tamed space. Let us define the set of
test functions
by

\displaystyle{\rm Test}(M):

\displaystyle=\{f\in D(\Delta)\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})\mid\Gamma(f)\in L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m}),\Delta f\in D(\mathscr{E})\},

\displaystyle{\rm Test}(M)_{fs}:

\displaystyle=\{f\in{\rm Test}(M)\mid\mathfrak{m}({\rm supp}[f])<\infty\}.

It is easy to see that
{\rm Test}(M)_{fs}\subset\mathscr{D}_{1,p}\subset H^{1,p}(M)
for any
p\in]1,+\infty[
.

Lemma 3.4

(
[
46
, Lemma 3.2]
)
.

Under Assumption
1
, for every
f\in{\rm Test}(M)
, we have

\Gamma(f)\in D(\mathscr{E})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
and there exists
\mu=\mu^{+}-\mu^{-}
with

\mu^{\pm}\in D(\mathscr{E})^{*}
such that

\displaystyle-\mathscr{E}^{2\kappa}(u,\varphi)=\int_{M}\tilde{\varphi}\,\mathrm{d}\mu\quad\text{ for all }\quad\varphi\in D(\mathscr{E}).

(3.2)

Moreover,
{\rm Test}(M)
is an algebra, i.e., for
f,g\in{\rm Test}(M)
,
fg\in{\rm Test}(M)
,
if further
f
\in{\rm Test}(M)^{n}
, then
\Phi(
f
)\in{\rm Test}(M)
for every smooth
function
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\Phi(0)=0
.

Lemma 3.5
.

{\rm Test}(M)\cap L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
is dense in
L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
both in
L^{p}
-norm
and in
L^{2}
-norm. In particular,
{\rm Test}(M)
is dense in
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
.

Proof.

Take
f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
. We may assume
f\in L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, because

f
is
L^{p}
(and also
L^{2}
)-approximated by a sequence
\{f^{k}\}
of

L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
-functions defined
by
f^{k}:=(-k)\lor f\land k
. If
f\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
,

P_{t}f\in{\rm Test}(M)
by Lemma
3.1
and

\{P_{t}f\}\subset{\rm Test}(M)\cap L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
converges to

f
in
L^{p}
and in
L^{2}
as
t\to 0
. If
f\in D(\mathscr{E}))
, then
f
can be approximated by

\{P_{t}f^{k}\}
in
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
. This shows the last statement.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

As proved above,
{\rm Test}(M)
forms an algebra and dense in
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
under
Assumption
1
. However,
{\rm Test}(M)
is not necessarily a subspace of
C_{b}(M)
.
When the tamed space comes from
{\rm RCD}
-space, the Sobolev-to-Lipschitz property of RCD-spaces ensures

{\rm Test}(M)\subset C_{b}(M)
.

4

Vector space calculus for tamed space

4.1

L^{\infty}
-module

We need the notion of
L^{p}
-normed
L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
-modules.

Definition 4.1

(
L^{\infty}
-module)
.

Given
p\in[1,+\infty]
, a real Banach space
(\mathscr{M},\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{M}})
, or simply,
\mathscr{M}
is called an
L^{p}
-normed
L^{\infty}
-module
(over
(M,\mathfrak{m})
) if it satisfies

(a)

a bilinear map
\cdot
:
L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})\times\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{M}
satisfying

\displaystyle(fg)\cdot v

\displaystyle=f\cdot(gv),

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{M}\cdot v

\displaystyle=v,

(b)

a nonnegatively valued map
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}:\mathscr{M}\to L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
such that

\displaystyle|f\cdot v|_{\mathfrak{m}}

\displaystyle=|f||v|_{\mathfrak{m}}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.},

\displaystyle\|v\|_{\mathscr{M}}

\displaystyle=\||v|_{\mathfrak{m}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})},

for every
f,g\in L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
and
v\in\mathscr{M}
. If only (a) is satisfied, we call

(\mathscr{M},\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{M}})
or simply
\mathscr{M}
an
L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
-module.

Throughout this paper, we always assume that for every
v\in\mathscr{M}
its point-wise norm
|v|_{\mathfrak{m}}
is Borel measurable.
We call
\mathscr{M}
an
L^{\infty}
-module
if it is
L^{p}
-normed
for some
p\in[1,+\infty]
.
\mathscr{M}
is called
separable

if it is a separable Banach space. We call
v\in\mathscr{M}
is
(
\mathfrak{m}
-essentially) bounded if
|v|_{\mathfrak{m}}\in L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
.
\mathscr{M}
is called
Hilbert module
if is an
L^{2}
-normed
L^{\infty}
-module, in this case, the point-wise norm
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}
satisfies a point-wise
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. parallelogram identity, hence it induces a
point-wise scalar product

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}:\mathscr{M}\times\mathscr{M}\to L^{1}(M;\mathfrak{m})
which is
L^{\infty}
-bilinear,
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. nonnegative definite, local in both components, satisfies the point-wise
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and reproduces the Hilbertian scalar product on
\mathscr{M}
by integration with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
.

Definition 4.2

(Dual module)
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{N}
be
L^{p}
-normed
L^{\infty}
-module. Denote both point-wise norms by
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}
. A map
T:\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{N}
is called
module morphism

if it is a bounded linear map in the sense of functional analysis and

\displaystyle T(f\,v)=f\,T(v)

(4.1)

for every
v\in\mathscr{M}
and every
f\in L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
. The set of all module morphisms
is written
{\rm Hom}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{N})
and equipped with the usual operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{M},\mathscr{N}}
. We call
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{N}

isomorphic
(as
L^{\infty}
-module) if there exists
T\in{\rm Hom}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{N})
and
S\in{\rm Hom}(\mathscr{N},\mathscr{M})
such that
T\circ S={\rm Id}_{\mathscr{N}}
and
S\circ T={\rm Id}_{\mathscr{M}}
.
Any such
T
is called
module isomorphism
. If in addition, such a
T
is a norm isometry, it is called
module isometric isomorphism
. In fact, by (
4.1
) every module isometric isomorphism
T
preserves pointwise norms
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e., i.e. for every
v\in\mathscr{M}
,

\displaystyle|T(v)|_{\mathfrak{m}}=|v|_{\mathfrak{m}}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

The dual module
\mathscr{M}^{*}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathscr{M}^{*}:={\rm Hom}(\mathscr{M},L^{1}(M;\mathfrak{m}))

and will be endowed with the usual operator norm. The point-wise paring between

v\in\mathscr{M}
and
L\in\mathscr{M}^{*}
is denoted by
L(v)\in L^{1}(M;\mathfrak{m})
. If
\mathscr{M}
is
L^{p}
-normed, then
\mathscr{M}^{*}
is an
L^{q}
-normed
L^{\infty}
-module, where
p,q\in[1,+\infty]
with
1/p+1/q=1
(see
[
27
, Proposition 1.2.14]
) with naturally defined multiplications and, and by a slight abuse of notation, point-wise norm is given by

\displaystyle|L|_{\mathfrak{m}}:=\mathfrak{m}\text{\rm-esssup}\{|L(v)|\mid v\in\mathscr{M},|v|_{\mathfrak{m}}\leq 1\;\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}\}.

(4.2)

By
[
27
, Corollary 1.2.16]
, if
p<\infty
,

\displaystyle|v|_{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathfrak{m}\text{\rm-esssup}\{|L(v)|\mid L\in\mathscr{M},|L|_{\mathfrak{m}}\leq 1\;\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}\}

for every
v\in\mathscr{M}
. Moreover, under
p<\infty
, in the sense of functional analysis

\mathscr{M}^{*}
and the dual Banach space
\mathscr{M}^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{M}
are isometrically isomorphic

[
27
, Proposition 1.2.13]
. In this case, the natural point-wise paring map
\mathscr{I}:\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{M}^{**}
, where

\mathscr{M}^{**}:={\rm Hom}(\mathscr{M}^{*},L^{1}(M;\mathfrak{m}))
, belongs to
{\rm Hom}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{M}^{**})
and constitutes a norm isometry is
L^{p}
-normed for
p\in]1,+\infty[
, this is equivalent to
\mathscr{M}
being reflexive as Banach space
[
27
, Corollary 1.2.18]
,
while for
p=1
, the implication from “reflexive as Banach space”  to “reflexive as
L^{\infty}
-module”  still holds
[
27
, Propositions 1.2.13 and 1.2.17]
. In particular all Hilbert modules are reflexive in both senses.

Definition 4.3

(
L^{0}
-module)
.

Fix an
L^{\infty}
-module
\mathscr{M}
. Let
(B_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
a Borel partition of
M
such that
\mathfrak{m}(B_{i})\in]0,+\infty[
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Denote by
\mathscr{M}^{0}
the completion of

\mathscr{M}
with respect to
\mathscr{M}
the distance
{\sf d}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}}:\mathscr{M}\times\mathscr{M}\to[0,+\infty[
defined by

\displaystyle{\sf d}_{\mathscr{M}^{0}}(v,w):=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{i}\mathfrak{m}(B_{i})}\int_{B_{i}}(|v-w|_{\mathfrak{m}}\land 1)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}.

We call
\mathscr{M}^{0}
as the
L^{0}
-module
associated with
\mathscr{M}
. The induced topology on
\mathscr{M}^{0}
does not depend on the choice of
(B_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
(see
[
27
, p. 31]
). In addition, scalar and functional multiplication, and the point-wise norm
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}
extend continuously to
\mathscr{M}^{0}
, so that all
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. properties mentioned for
L^{\infty}
-module hold for general elements in
\mathscr{M}^{0}
and
L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
in place of
\mathscr{M}
and
L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
. The point-wise paring of
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{M}^{*}
extends uniquely and continuously to a bilinear map on
\mathscr{M}\times(\mathscr{M}^{*})^{0}
with values in
L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
such that for every
v\in\mathscr{M}^{0}
and every
L\in(\mathscr{M}^{*})^{0}
,

\displaystyle|L(v)|\leq|L|_{\mathfrak{m}}|v|_{\mathfrak{m}}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

We have the following characterization of elements in
(\mathscr{M}^{*})^{0}
:

Proposition 4.4

(
[
27
, Proposition 1.3.2]
)
.

Let
T:\mathscr{M}^{0}\to L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
be a linear map for which there exists
f\in L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
such that
for every
v\in\mathscr{M}
,

\displaystyle|T(v)|\leq f|v|_{\mathfrak{m}}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

Then there exists a unique
L\in(\mathscr{M}^{*})^{0}
such that for every
v\in\mathscr{M}
,

\displaystyle L(v)=T(v)\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.,}

and we furthermore have

\displaystyle|L|_{\mathfrak{m}}\leq f\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

4.2

Tensor products

Definition 4.5

(Tensor products)
.

Let
\mathscr{M}_{1}
and
\mathscr{M}_{2}
be two Hilbert module.
By a slight abuse of notation,
we denote both point-wise scalar products by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}
.
We consider the
L^{0}
-module
\mathscr{M}_{i}^{0}
induced from
\mathscr{M}_{i}
,
i=1,2
.
Let
\mathscr{M}_{1}^{0}\odot\mathscr{M}_{2}^{0}
be the “algebraic tensor product”
consisting of all finite linear combinations of formal elements
v\otimes w
,
v\in\mathscr{M}_{1}^{0}
and
w\in\mathscr{M}_{2}^{0}
, obtained by factorizing appropriate vector spaces (see
[
27
, Section 1.5]
). It naturally comes with a multiplications
\cdot\,:L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})\times\mathscr{M}_{1}^{0}\odot\mathscr{M}_{2}^{0}\to\mathscr{M}_{1}^{0}\odot\mathscr{M}_{2}^{0}
defined through

\displaystyle f(v\otimes w):=(fv)\otimes w=v\otimes(fw)

and a point-wise scalar product
\langle\cdot\,|\,\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}\,:(\mathscr{M}_{1}^{0}\odot\mathscr{M}_{2}^{0})^{2}\to L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
given by

\displaystyle\langle(v_{1}\otimes w_{1})\,|\,(v_{2}\otimes w_{2})\rangle:=\langle v_{1},v_{2}\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}\langle w_{1},w_{2}\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}},

both extended to
\mathscr{M}_{1}^{0}\odot\mathscr{M}_{2}^{0}
by bi-linearity.
Then
\langle\cdot\,|\,\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}
is bilinear,
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. non-negative definite, symmetric, and local in both components
(see
[
30
, Lemma 3.2.19]
).

The point-wise
Hilbert-Schmidt norm

|\cdot|_{{\rm HS}}:\mathscr{M}_{1}^{0}\odot\mathscr{M}_{2}^{0}\to L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
is given by

\displaystyle|A|_{{\rm HS}}:=\sqrt{\langle A\,|\,A\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}}.

This map satisfies the
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. triangle inequality and is
1
-homogenous with respect to multiplication with
L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
-functions (see
[
27
, p. 44]
).
Consequently, the map
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{M}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{M}_{2}}:\mathscr{M}_{1}^{0}\odot\,\mathscr{M}_{2}^{0}\to[0,+\infty]
defined through

\displaystyle\|A\|_{\mathscr{M}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{M}_{2}}:=\||A|_{{\rm HS},\mathfrak{m}}\|_{L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})}

has all properties of a norm except that it might take the value
+\infty
.

Now we define the
tensor product

\mathscr{M}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{M}_{2}
the
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{M}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{M}_{2}}
-completion of the subspace that consists of all
A\in\mathscr{M}_{1}^{0}\odot\mathscr{M}_{2}^{0}
such that
\|A\|_{\mathscr{M}_{1}\otimes\mathscr{M}_{2}}langle\infty
.
Denote by
A^{\top}\in\mathscr{M}^{\otimes 2}
the
transpose
of
A\in\mathscr{M}^{\otimes 2}
as defined in

[
27
, Section 1.5]
. For instance, for bounded
v,w\in\mathscr{M}
we have

\displaystyle(v\otimes w)^{\top}=w\otimes v.

(4.3)

4.3

Exterior products

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a Hilbert module and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.
Set
\Lambda^{0}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}:=L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
and, for
k\geq 1
, let
\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
be the “exterior product”constructed by suitable factorizing
(\mathscr{M}^{0})^{\odot k}
(see
[
27
, Section 1.5]
).
The representative of
v_{1}\odot\cdots\odot v_{k},v_{1},\cdots,v_{k}\in\mathscr{M}^{0}
in
\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
is written
v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{k}
.
\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
naturally comes with a multiplication
\cdot:L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})\times\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
via

\displaystyle f(v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{k}):=(fv_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{k}=\cdots=v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge(fv_{k})

and a pointwise scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}:(\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0})^{2}\to L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
defined by

\displaystyle\langle v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{k},w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{k}\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}:={\rm det}\left[\langle v_{i},w_{j}\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}\right]_{i,j\in\{1,2,\cdots,k\}}

(4.4)

up to a factor
k!
, both extended to
\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
by (bi-)linearity. Then
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}
is bilinear,

\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. non-negative definite, symmetric, and local in both components.

Given any
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the map assigning to
v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{k}\in\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
and

w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{k^{\prime}}\in\Lambda^{k^{\prime}}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
the element

v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{k}\wedge w_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{k^{\prime}}\in\Lambda^{k+k^{\prime}}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
can and will be uniquely extended by bilinearity and continuity to a bilinear map

\bigwedge:\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}\times\Lambda^{k^{\prime}}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
termed
wedge product
(see
[
30
, p. 47]
).
If
k=0
or
k^{\prime}=0
, it simply corresponds to multiplication of elements of
\Lambda^{k^{\prime}}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
or

\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
, respectively, with function in
L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
according to (
4.4
).

By a slight abuse of notation, define the map
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}:\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}\to L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
by

\displaystyle|\omega|_{\mathfrak{m}}:=\sqrt{\langle\omega,\omega\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}}.

It obeys the
\mathfrak{m}
-a.e. triangle inequality and is homogenous with respect to multiplication with
L^{0}(M;\mathfrak{m})
-functions (see
[
30
, p. 47]
).

It follows that the map
\|\cdot\|_{\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}}:=\||\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}\|_{L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})}

has all properties of a norm except that
\|w\|_{\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}}
might be infinite.

Definition 4.6
.

The (
k
-fold) exterior product
\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}
is defined as the completion with respect to

\|\cdot\|_{\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}}
of the subspace consisting of all
\omega\in\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}^{0}
such that
\|\omega\|_{\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}}
.

The space
\Lambda^{k}\!\mathscr{M}
naturally becomes a Hilbert module and, if
\mathscr{M}
is separable, is separable as wel (see
[
30
, p. 47]
).

4.4

Cotangent module

Let
(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
be a quasi-regular strongly local Dirichlet form on
L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
.
We define the
cotangent module

L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
, i.e., the space of differential

1
-forms that are
L^{2}
-integrable in a certain “universal”  sense.

Definition 4.7

(Pre-cotangent module)
.

We define the
pre-cotangent module

{\rm Pcm}
by

\displaystyle{\rm Pcm}:

\displaystyle=\Biggl\{\left.(f_{i},A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\;\Biggr|\,(A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\text{ Borel partition of }X,\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subset D(\mathscr{E})_{e},\;\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{A_{i}}\Gamma(f_{i})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}<\infty\right\}

Moreover, we define a relation
\sim
on
{\rm Pcm}
by
(f_{i},A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sim(g_{j},B_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
if and only if
\int_{A_{i}\cap B_{j}}\Gamma(f_{i}-g_{j})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}=0
for every
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
. The relation, in fact forms an equivalence relation by
[
12
, §2.1]
.
The equivalence class of an element
(f_{i},A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\in{\rm Pcm}
with respect to
\sim
is denoted by
[f_{i},A_{i}]
. The space
{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
of equivalence classes becomes a vector space via the well-defined operations

\displaystyle[f_{i},A_{i}]+[g_{j},B_{j}]:=[f_{i}+g_{j},A_{i}\cap B_{j}],\qquad\lambda[f_{i},A_{i}]:=[\lambda f_{i},A_{i}]

(4.5)

for every
[f_{i},A_{i}],[g_{j},B_{j}]\in{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

Now we define the space
{\rm SF}(M;\mathfrak{m})\subset L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
of simple functions, i.e., each element

h\in{\rm SF}(M;\mathfrak{m})
attains only a finite number values. For
[f_{i},A_{i}]\in{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim

and
h=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}a_{j}{\bf 1}_{B_{j}}\in{\rm SF}(M;\mathfrak{m})
with a Borel partition
(B_{j})
of
M
, we define the product

h[f_{i},A_{i}]\in{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
as

\displaystyle h[f_{i},A_{i}]:=[a_{j}f_{i},A_{i}\cap B_{j}],

(4.6)

where we set
B_{j}:=\emptyset
and
a_{j}:=0
for every
j>{\ell}
.
It is readily verified that this definition is well-posed and that the resulting multiplication is a bilinear map from
{\rm SF}(M;\mathfrak{m})\times{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
into
{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
such that for every

[f_{i},A_{i}]\in{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
and every
h,k\in{\rm SF}(M;\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle(hk)[f_{i},A_{i}]=h(k[f_{i},A_{i}]),\qquad{\bf 1}[f_{i},A_{i}]=[f_{i},A_{i}].

(4.7)

Moreover, the map
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)}:{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim\,\to[0,+\infty[
given by

\displaystyle\|[f_{i},A_{i}]\|_{L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)}^{2}:=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{A_{i}}\Gamma(f_{i})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}<\infty

constitutes a norm on
{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
.

Definition 4.8

(Cotangent module)
.

We define the Banach space
(L^{2}(T^{*}\!M),\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)})
as the completion of
({\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim,\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)})
. The pair
(L^{2}(T^{*}\!M),\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)})
or simply

L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
is called
cotangent module
, and the elements of
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
are called
cotangent vector fields
or (
differential
)
1
-forms
.

The following are shown in
[
12
, Lemma 2.3 and Theorem 2.4]
:

Lemma 4.9

(
[
12
, Lemma 2.3]
)
.

The map from
{\rm SF}(M)\times{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
into
{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
defined in (
4.6
)
extends continuously and unniquely to a bilinear map from
L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})\times L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
into
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
satisfying, for every
f,g\in L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
and every
\omega\in L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
,

\displaystyle(fg)\omega=f(g\omega),\quad{\bf 1}_{M}\omega=\omega,\quad\|f\omega\|_{L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})}\cdot\|\omega\|_{L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)}.

Theorem 4.10

(Module property,
[
12
, Theorem 2.4]
)
.

The cotangent module
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
is an
L^{2}
-normed
L^{\infty}
-module over
M
with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
whose point-wise norm
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}
satisfies, for every
[f_{i},A_{i}]\in{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim
,

\displaystyle|[f_{i},A_{i}]|_{\mathfrak{m}}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}{\bf 1}_{A_{i}}\Gamma(f_{i})^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

(4.8)

In particular,
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
is a Hilbert module with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
.

Definition 4.11

(
L^{2}
-differential)
.

The
L^{2}
-differential
\mathrm{d}f
of any function
f\in D(\mathscr{E})_{e}
is defined by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}f}:=[f,X]\in L^{2}(T^{*}\!M),

where
[f,X]\in{\rm Pcm}/\!\!\sim\;\subset L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)

is the representative of the sequence
(f_{i},A_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
given by

f_{i}:=f
,
A_{1}:=X
,
f_{i}:=0
and
A_{i}:=\emptyset
for every
i\geq 2
.

As usual, we call a
1
-form
\omega\in L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)

exact
if, for some
f\in D(\mathscr{E})_{e}
,

\displaystyle\omega=\mathrm{d}f.

The
L^{2}
-differential
\mathrm{d}
is a linear operator on
D(\mathscr{E})_{e}
. By (
4.8
), the
L^{\infty}
-module structure induced by
\mathfrak{m}
according to Theorem
4.10
,

\displaystyle|\mathrm{d}f|_{\mathfrak{m}}=\Gamma(f)^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

holds for every
f\in D(\mathscr{E})_{e}
.

4.5

Tangent module

In this section, let
(M,{\sf d},\mathfrak{m})
be a metric measure space and assume the
infinitesimally Hilbertian condition for it. In particular, we are given a strongly local Dirichlet form

(\mathscr{E},D(\mathscr{E}))
on
L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
.
We define the notion of
tangent module
.

Definition 4.12

(Tangent module)
.

The tangent module
(L^{2}(TM),\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(TM)})
or simply
L^{2}(TM)
is

\displaystyle L^{2}(TM):=L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)^{*}

and it is endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(TM)}
induced by (
4.2
).
The elements of
L^{2}(TM)
will be called
vector fields
.

As in Subsection
4.1
, the point-wise pairing between
\omega\in L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
and
X\in L^{2}(TM)
is denoted by
\omega(X)\in L^{1}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, and, by a slight abuse of notation,

|X|\in L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
denotes the point-wise norm of
M
. By
[
12
, Lemma 2.7 and Proposition 1.24]
,

L^{2}(TM)
is a separable Hilbert module. Furthermore, in terms of the point-wise scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{2}(TM)
, respectively,
[
12
, Proposition 1.24]
allows us to define the
(Riesz) musical isomorphisms

\sharp:L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)\to L^{2}(TM)

and
\flat:=\sharp^{-1}
defined by

\displaystyle\langle\omega^{\sharp},X\rangle:=\omega(X)=:\langle X^{\flat},\omega\rangle\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

(4.9)

Definition 4.13

(
L^{2}
-gradient)
.

The
L^{2}
-gradient

\nabla f
of a function
f\in D(\mathscr{E})_{e}
is defined by

\displaystyle\nabla f:=(\mathrm{d}f)^{\sharp}.

Observe from (
4.9
) that
f\in D(\mathscr{E})_{e}
, is characterized as the unique element

X\in L^{2}(TM)
which satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}f(X)=|\mathrm{d}f|^{2}=|X|^{2}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

4.6

Divergences

Definition 4.14

(
L^{2}
-divergence)
.

We define the space
D({\rm div})\subset L^{2}(TM)
by

\displaystyle D({\rm div}):

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{X\in L^{2}(TM)\,\Bigl|\,\text{ there exists a function }f\in L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\text{ such that for every }h\in D(\mathscr{E}),\quad-\int_{M}h\,f\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}=\int_{M}\mathrm{d}h(X)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}\Bigr\}.

Such
f\in L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
is unique, and it is called the
(
L^{2}
-
)
divergence of

M
and denoted by
{\rm div}\,X
.

The uniqueness of
{\rm div}\,X
comes from the denseness of
D(\mathscr{E})
in
L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
.
Note that the map
{\rm div}:D({\rm div})\to L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
is linear, hence
D({\rm div})
is a vector space. By definition of
D(\Delta)
,
\nabla D(\Delta)\subset D({\rm div})
and

\displaystyle{\rm div}\nabla f=\Delta f\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

(4.10)

for every
f\in D(\Delta)
. Moreover, using the Leibniz rule in
[
30
, Theorem 4.4]
,
one can verify that for every
X\in D({\rm div})
and
f\in D(\mathscr{E})_{e}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
with

|\mathrm{d}f|\in L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, we have
fX\in D({\rm div})
and

\displaystyle{\rm div}(fX)=f\,{\rm div}(X)+\mathrm{d}f(X)\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

(4.11)

Definition 4.15

(Measure-valued divergence)
.

We define the space
D({\bf div})\subset L^{2}(TM)
by

\displaystyle D({\bf div}):

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{X\in L^{2}(TM)\,\Bigl|\,\text{ there exists a $\sigma$-finite signed Radon smooth measure }\nu

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ such that for every }h\in D(\mathscr{E})_{c}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m}),\quad-\int_{M}h\,\mathrm{d}\nu=\int_{M}\mathrm{d}h(X)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}\Bigr\}.

(4.12)

Such a
\sigma
-finite signed Radon smooth measure
\nu
is unique, and it is called the
measure-valued divergence
of
X
and denoted by
{\bf div}\,X
.
Moreover, we define the subclass
D_{f}({\bf div})
of
D({\bf div})
as follows:

\displaystyle D_{f}({\bf div}):

\displaystyle=\Bigl\{X\in L^{2}(TM)\,\Bigl|\,\text{ there exists a finite signed smooth measure }\nu

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ such that for every }h\in D(\mathscr{E})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m}),\quad-\int_{M}h\,\mathrm{d}\nu=\int_{M}\mathrm{d}h(X)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}\Bigr\}.

(4.13)

Such a finite signed smooth measure
\nu
is unique, and it is also called the
measure-valued divergence
of
X
and denoted by
{\bf div}\,X
.

The following Leibniz rule is an improvement of
[
12
, Lemma 3.13]
and is useful.

Lemma 4.16
.

For every
X\in D({\bf div})

(
resp.
X\in D_{f}({\bf div})
)
and every
f\in D(\mathscr{E})_{e}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, we have

fX\in D({\bf div})

(
resp.
fX\in D_{f}({\bf div})
)
with

\displaystyle{\bf div}(fX)=\tilde{f}{\bf div}\,X+\mathrm{d}f(X)\mathfrak{m}.

Proof.

We only show the proof for the case
X\in D({\bf div})
. The proof for the case
X\in D_{f}({\bf div})
is similar.
It is easy to see
fX\in L^{2}(TM)
. Moreover
(\mathrm{d}f)(X)=\langle\nabla f,X\rangle\in L^{1}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, since
X\in D({\bf div})
and
f\in D(\mathscr{E})_{e}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
. Then
(\mathrm{d}f)(X)\mathfrak{m}
is a finite signed smooth measure, hence
a
\sigma
-finite signed Radon smooth measure. Then the measure
\nu
defined by

\displaystyle\nu:=\tilde{f}{\bf div}\,X+\mathrm{d}f(X)\mathfrak{m}

is also a signed Radon smooth measure, because
\tilde{f}
is bounded. For
h\in D(\mathscr{E})_{c}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
,

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\tilde{h}\,\mathrm{d}\nu

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\tilde{h}\tilde{f}\,\mathrm{d}{\bf div}\,X-\int_{M}h\,\mathrm{d}f(X)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\mathrm{d}(hf)(X)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}-\int_{M}h\mathrm{d}f(X)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}(\mathrm{d}(hf)-h\mathrm{d}f)(X)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}f\mathrm{d}h(X)\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}=\int_{M}\mathrm{d}h(fX)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m},

which implies
\nu={\bf div}\,fX
. Here we use the Leibniz rule (see
[
12
, Proposition 2.11]
):
For every
f,g\in D(\mathscr{E})_{e}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}(fg)=\tilde{f}\,\mathrm{d}g+\tilde{g}\,\mathrm{d}f.

∎

4.7

Measure-valued Laplacian

Definition 4.17

(Measure-valued Schrödinger operator)
.

We define
D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa})
to consist of all
u\in D(\mathscr{E})
for which there exists a

\sigma
-finite Radon signed smooth measure
\nu
such that for
D(\mathscr{E})\subset L^{1}(M;|\nu|)
and
for every
h\in D(\mathscr{E})
, we have

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\tilde{h}\mathrm{d}\nu=\mathscr{E}^{2\kappa}(h,u).

In case of existence,
\nu
is unique, denoted by
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa}u
and shall be called the

measure-valued Schrödinger operator
with potential
2\kappa
.
We define

\displaystyle D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa}):=\{u\in\text{$D(\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}^{2\kappa})$}\mid{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa}u\text{ is a finite signed smooth measure }\}.

Definition 4.18

(Measure-valued Laplacian)
.

We define
D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
to consist of all
u\in D(\mathscr{E})
for which there exists a

\sigma
-finite Radon signed smooth measure
\nu
such that
for every
h\in D(\mathscr{E})_{c}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, we have

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\tilde{h}\mathrm{d}\nu=\mathscr{E}(h,u).

In case of existence,
\nu
is unique, denoted by
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u
and shall be called the

measure-valued Laplacian
. In another word,

D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
consists of all
u\in D(\mathscr{E})
for which
\nabla u\in D({\bf div})
, and
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u:={\bf div}\nabla u
. The subspace

D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})(\subset\text{$D(\text{\boldmath$\Delta$})$})

such that
the
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u
forms a finite signed smooth measure for
u\in D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})

can be
similarly defined by replacing the test functions
h\in D(\mathscr{E})_{c}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
with

h\in D(\mathscr{E})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
. In particular,
D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
consists of all

u\in D(\mathscr{E})
for which
\nabla u\in D_{f}({\bf div})
.
For
u\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
,
denote by
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}_{\ll}u
(resp.
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}_{\perp}u
) the absolutely continuous (resp. singular) part of
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u
with respect to
\mathfrak{m}
and set

\displaystyle\Delta_{\ll}u:=\frac{\mathrm{d}{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}_{\ll}u}{\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}}.

Lemma 4.19

(cf.
[
12
, Lemma 8.12]
)
.

The signed Borel measure
\tilde{u}^{2}\kappa
for
u\in D(\mathscr{E})
has finite total variation.
For
u\in D(\mathscr{E})
with
u^{2}\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa})

(
resp.
u^{2}\in D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa})
)
, we have
u^{2}\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})

(
resp.
u^{2}\in D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
)
with

\displaystyle{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa}u^{2}={\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u^{2}-2\tilde{u}^{2}\kappa.

Proof.

Since
|\kappa|\in S_{D}({\bf X})
, we see that
\tilde{u}^{2}|\kappa|
and
\tilde{u}^{2}\kappa^{\pm}
are finite smooth measures in view of Stollmann-Voigt’s inequality (
1.1
).
Since
(M,\tau)
is a Lusin topological space, any finite measure is a Radon smooth measure. Thus,
\tilde{u}^{2}\kappa
is a
signed Radon smooth measure.
For
u\in D(\mathscr{E})
with
u^{2}\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa})
(resp.
u^{2}\in D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa})
), if we set
\nu:={\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa}u^{2}+2\tilde{u}^{2}\kappa
, then
\nu

is a signed Radon (resp. finite) smooth measure, and for
h\in D(\mathscr{E})_{c}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
(resp.
h\in D(\mathscr{E})\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
), we have

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\tilde{h}\,\mathrm{d}\nu

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\tilde{h}\,\mathrm{d}{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}^{2\kappa}u^{2}-2\int_{M}\tilde{h}\tilde{u}^{2}\mathrm{d}\kappa

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}^{2\kappa}(h,u^{2})-\langle 2\kappa,\tilde{h}\tilde{u}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathscr{E}(h,u^{2}),

hence
u^{2}\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
(resp.
u^{2}\in D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
) and
\nu={\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u^{2}
.
∎

Lemma 4.20
.

Take
f\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
. Let
\Phi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
satisfy
\Phi(0)=0
,
\Phi^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime}\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then
\Phi(f)\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
with

\displaystyle{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}\Phi(f)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\prime\prime}(f)|\nabla f|^{2}\mathfrak{m}+\Phi^{\prime}(f){\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}f,

(4.14)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\ll}\Phi(f)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\prime\prime}(f)|\nabla f|^{2}+\Phi^{\prime}(f)\Delta_{\ll}f.

(4.15)

If further
f\in D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
, then
\Phi(f)\in D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
with (
4.14
) and (
4.15
).

Proof.

It is easy to see
\Phi(f)\in D(\mathscr{E})
by
\Phi(0)=0
. Take
h\in D(\mathscr{E})_{c}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
. Then, by way of the Leibniz rule for measure-valued divergence (see Lemma
4.16
),

\displaystyle-\mathscr{E}(h,\Phi(f))

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\langle\nabla h,\nabla\Phi(f)\rangle\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\langle\nabla h,\Phi^{\prime}(f)\nabla f\rangle\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\tilde{h}\,\mathrm{d}\,{\bf div}(\Phi^{\prime}(f)\nabla f)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\tilde{h}\,\mathrm{d}\left(\Phi^{\prime}(f){\bf div}(\nabla f)+\mathrm{d}\Phi^{\prime}(f)(\nabla f)\right)\quad(\text{Lemma~\ref{lem:LeibnizRule}})

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\tilde{h}\,\mathrm{d}\left(\Phi^{\prime}(f){\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}f+\Phi^{\prime\prime}(f)|\nabla f|^{2}\mathfrak{m}\right),

which implies
\Phi(f)\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
and
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}\Phi(f)=\Phi^{\prime}(f){\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}f+\Phi^{\prime\prime}(f)|\nabla f|^{2}\mathfrak{m}
. The proof of the latter assertion is similar.
∎

Definition 4.21
.

We define
\dot{D}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}
to consist of all
u\in\dot{D}(\mathscr{E})_{{\rm loc}}
for which there exists a
\sigma
-finite signed Radon smooth measure
\nu
such that for every
h\in\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}D(\mathscr{E})_{G_{k}}\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
with
\{G_{k}\}\in\Xi(u)
, we have

\displaystyle-\int_{M}\tilde{h}\,\mathrm{d}\nu=\mathscr{E}(h,u).

In case of existence,
\nu
is unique, denoted by
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u
and shall be called the

measure-valued Laplacian
.
We define the subspace
\dot{D}_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}
of
\dot{D}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}
by

\displaystyle\dot{D}_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}:=\{u\in\dot{D}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}\mid{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u\text{ is a finite signed smooth measure}\}.

It is easy to see
D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})\subset\dot{D}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}
,

D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})\subset\dot{D}_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}
and

1\in\dot{D}_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}
with
{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}1=0
.

Corollary 4.22
.

Take
{\varepsilon}>0
and
p\in[1,2]
. If
u\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{+}

(
resp.
u\in D_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{+}
)
, then
(u+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}\in\dot{D}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}

(
resp.
(u+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}\in\dot{D}_{f}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}
)

and

\displaystyle{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}(u+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{2}\left(\frac{p}{2}-1\right)\left(u+{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\mathfrak{m}+\frac{p}{2}(\tilde{u}+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}-1}{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u,

(4.16)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\ll}(u+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{2}\left(\frac{p}{2}-1\right)\left(u+{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}-2}|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{p}{2}(\tilde{u}+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}-1}\Delta_{\ll}u\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

(4.17)

Proof.

Take
\Psi(r):=(r+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}-{\varepsilon}^{p}
for
r\geq 0
.
\Psi
can be extended to a
C^{2}
-function on
\mathbb{R}
with
\Psi(0)=0
,
\Psi^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime\prime}\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
. Then we have
\Psi(u)\in D({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})
and

\displaystyle{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}\Psi(u)=\frac{p}{2}\left(\frac{p}{2}-1\right)\left(u+{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)^{\frac{p}{2}-2}|\nabla u|^{2}\mathfrak{m}+\frac{p}{2}(\tilde{u}+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}-1}{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}u.

In particular,
(u+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}=\Psi(u)+{\varepsilon}^{p}\in\dot{D}({\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}})_{{\rm loc}}
and

{\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}(u+{\varepsilon}^{2})^{\frac{p}{2}}={\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}(\Psi(u)+{\varepsilon}^{p})={\text{\boldmath$\Delta$}}\Psi(u)
.
∎

4.8

The Lebesgue spaces
L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{p}(TM)

Let
L^{0}(T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{0}(TM)
be the
L^{0}
-modules as in Definition
4.3
associated to
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{2}(TM)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle L^{0}(T^{*}\!M):=L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)^{0},\quad L^{0}(TM):=L^{2}(TM)^{0}.

The characterization of Cauchy sequences in these spaces grants that
the point-wise norms
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}:L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)\to L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
and
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}:L^{2}(TM)\to L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m})
as well as the (Riesz) musical isomorphisms
\flat:L^{2}(TM)\to L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
and
\sharp:L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)\to L^{2}(TM)
uniquely extend to (non-relabeled) continuous map
\flat:L^{0}(TM)\to L^{0}(T^{*}\!M)
and
\sharp:L^{0}(T^{*}\!M)\to L^{0}(TM)
.

For
p\in[1,+\infty]
, let
L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{p}(TM)
be the Banach spaces consisting of all
\omega\in L^{0}(T^{*}\!M)
and
X\in L^{0}(TM)
such that
|\omega|\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
and
|X|\in L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, respectively, endowed with the norms

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)}:=\||\omega|_{\mathfrak{m}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})},\qquad\|X\|_{L^{p}(TM)}:=\||X|_{\mathfrak{m}}\|_{L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})}.

Since by
[
12
, Lemma 2.7]
,
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
is separable, and so is
L^{2}(TM)
by
[
12
, Proposition 1.24]
. Then one easily derives that if
p\neq+\infty
, the spaces

L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{p}(TM)
are separable as well. Since
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{2}(TM)
are reflexive as Hilbert spaces, by the discussion in Definition
4.2
it follows that
L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{p}(TM)
are reflexive for every
p\in]1,+\infty[
. For
q\in[1,+\infty[
such that
1/p+1/q=1
in the sense of
L^{\infty}
-modules we have the duality

\displaystyle L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)^{*}=L^{q}(TM).

The point-wise norms
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}:L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)\to L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
and
|\cdot|_{\mathfrak{m}}:L^{p}(TM)\to L^{p}(M;\mathfrak{m})
as well as the (Riesz) musical isomorphisms
\flat:L^{p}(TM)\to L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
and
\sharp:L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)\to L^{p}(TM)
are defined by

\displaystyle{}_{L^{p}(TM)}\langle\omega^{\sharp},X\rangle_{L^{q}(TM)}:=\omega(X)=:{}_{L^{q}(T^{*}\!M)}\langle X^{\flat},\omega\rangle_{L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

(4.18)

for
X\in L^{q}(TM)
and
\omega\in L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
,
and we write (
4.18
) by

\displaystyle\langle\omega^{\sharp},X\rangle:=\omega(X)=:\langle X^{\flat},\omega\rangle\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

(4.19)

for simplicity.

4.9

Test and regular objects

Definition 4.23

(
{\rm Test}(TM)
and
{\rm Reg}(TM)
)
.

We define the subclass of
L^{2}(TM)
consisting of
test vector fields
or
regular vector fields
, respectively:

\displaystyle{\rm Test}(TM)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\nabla f_{i}\,\right|\,n\in\mathbb{N},f_{i},g_{i}\in{\rm Test}(M)\right\},

\displaystyle{\rm Reg}(TM)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\nabla f_{i}\,\right|\,n\in\mathbb{N},f_{i}\in{\rm Test}(M),g_{i}\in{\rm Test}(M)\cup\mathbb{R}{\bf 1}_{M}\right\}.

Definition 4.24

(
{\rm Test}(T^{*}\!M)
and
{\rm Reg}(T^{*}\!M)
)
.

We define the subclass of
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
consisting of
test
1
-forms
or
regular
1
-forms
, respectively:

\displaystyle{\rm Test}(T^{*}\!M)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\mathrm{d}f_{i}\,\right|\,n\in\mathbb{N},f_{i},g_{i}\in{\rm Test}(M)\right\},

\displaystyle{\rm Reg}(T^{*}\!M)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}g_{i}\mathrm{d}f_{i}\,\right|\,n\in\mathbb{N},f_{i}\in{\rm Test}(M),g_{i}\in{\rm Test}(M)\cup\mathbb{R}{\bf 1}_{M}\right\}.

{\rm Test}(TM)
(resp.
{\rm Test}(T^{*}\!M)
) is a dense subspace of
L^{p}(TM)
for
p\in[1,+\infty[
(resp.
L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
) (see
[
39
]
), hence

{\rm Reg}(TM)\cap L^{p}(TM)
(resp.
{\rm Reg}(T^{*}\!M)\cap L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
) is dense in
L^{p}(TM)
(resp.
L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
).
From this, we have that
L^{2}(TM)\cap L^{p}(TM)
(resp.
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)\cap L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
) is dense in
L^{p}(TM)

(resp.
L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)
) for
p\in[1,+\infty[
.

4.10

Lebesgue spaces on tensor products

Denote the two-fold tensor products of
L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{2}(TM)
, respectively, in the sense of Definition
4.5
by

\displaystyle L^{2}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X):=L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)\otimes L^{2}(T^{*}\!M),\quad L^{2}((T)^{\otimes 2}X):=L^{2}(TM)\otimes L^{2}(TM).

By the discussion from Subsection
4.2
, Theorem
4.10
and

[
12
, Proposition 1.24]
, both are separable modules. They are point-wise isometrically module isomorphic: the respective pairing is initially defined by

\displaystyle(\omega_{1}\otimes\omega_{2})(X_{1}\otimes X_{2}):=\omega_{1}(X_{1})\omega_{2}(X_{2})\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

for
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in L^{2}(T^{*}\!M)\cap L^{\infty}(T^{*}\!M)
and
X_{1},X_{2}\in L^{2}(TM)\cap L^{\infty}(TM)
,
and is extended by linearity and continuity to
L^{2}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
and
L^{2}((T)^{\otimes 2}X)
, respectively. By a slight abuse of notation, this pairing, with
[
12
, Proposition 1.24]
, induces the (Riesz) musical isomorphisms
\flat:L^{2}((T)^{\otimes 2}X)\to L^{2}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
and
\sharp:=\flat^{-1}
given by

\displaystyle\langle A^{\sharp}\,|\,T\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}:=A(T)=:\langle A\,|\,T^{\flat}\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

(4.20)

and
write
|A|_{\rm HS}:=\sqrt{\langle A\,|\,A\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}}
and
|T|_{\rm HS}:=\sqrt{\langle T\,|\,T\rangle_{\mathfrak{m}}}
for
A\in L^{2}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
and
T\in L^{2}((T)^{\otimes 2}X)
.

We let
L^{p}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
and
L^{p}(T^{\otimes 2}X)
,
p\in\{0\}\cup[1,+\infty]
, can be defined similarly as in
Subsection
4.8
. For
p\in[1,+\infty]
, these spaces naturally become Banach space which, if
p<\infty
, are separable. The following coincidence also holds

\displaystyle L^{p}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)=L^{p}(T^{*}\!M)\otimes L^{p}(T^{*}\!M),\quad L^{q}((T)^{\otimes 2}X):=L^{q}(TM)\otimes L^{q}(TM)

and the (Riesz) musical isomorphisms
\flat:L^{q}((T)^{\otimes 2}X)\to L^{p}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
and
\sharp:=\flat^{-1}
can be defined by
(
4.20
)
for
A\in L^{p}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
and
T\in L^{q}((T)^{\otimes 2}X)
.

We define the
L^{p}
-dense sets,
p\in[1,+\infty]
, intended strongly if
p<\infty
and weakly* if
p=\infty
, reminiscent of
Subsection
4.2
.

\displaystyle{\rm Test}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X):={\rm Test}(T^{*}\!M)^{\odot 2},

\displaystyle{\rm Test}(T^{\otimes 2}X):={\rm Test}(TM)^{\odot 2},

\displaystyle{\rm Reg}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X):={\rm Reg}(T^{*}\!M)^{\odot 2},

\displaystyle{\rm Reg}(T^{\otimes 2}X):={\rm Reg}(TM)^{\odot 2}.

But the denseness in
L^{p}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
(resp.
L^{p}(TM)^{\otimes 2}
)
for
{\rm Reg}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
(resp.
{\rm Reg}(T^{\otimes 2}X)
) should be understood as
for
{\rm Reg}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)\cap L^{p}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
(resp.
{\rm Reg}(T^{\otimes 2}X)\cap L^{p}(T^{\otimes 2}X)
).

4.11

Lebesgue spaces on exterior products

Given any
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, we set

\displaystyle L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M):

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{k}L^{2}(T^{*}\!M),

\displaystyle L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}TM):

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{k}L^{2}(TM),

where the exterior products are defined in Subsection
4.3
. For
k\in\{0,1\}
, we see

\displaystyle L^{2}(\Lambda^{1}T^{*}\!M)

\displaystyle=L^{2}(T^{*}\!M),

\displaystyle L^{2}(\Lambda^{1}TM)

\displaystyle=L^{2}(TM),

\displaystyle L^{2}(\Lambda^{0}T^{*}\!M)

\displaystyle=L^{2}(\Lambda^{0}TM)=L^{2}(M;\mathfrak{m}).

By Subsection
4.3
, these are naturally Hilbert modules. As in Subsection
4.10
,

L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}TM)
are pointwise isometrically module isomorphic.
For brevity, the induced pointwise pairing between
\omega\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)
and
X_{1}\land\cdots\land X_{k}\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}TM)
with
X_{1},\cdots,X_{k}\in L^{2}(TM)\cap L^{\infty}(TM)
, is written by

\displaystyle\omega(X_{1},\cdots,X_{k}):=\omega(X_{1}\land\cdots\land X_{k}).

We let
L^{p}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)
and
L^{p}(\Lambda^{k}TM)
,
p\in\{0\}\cup[1,+\infty]
, be as in Subsection
4.8
.
For
p\in[1,+\infty]
, these spaces are Banach and, if
p<\infty
, additionally separable.
We define the formal
k
-th exterior products,
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, of the classes from Subsection
4.9
as follows:

\displaystyle{\rm Test}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M):

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}^{0}\mathrm{d}f_{i}^{1}\land\cdots\land\mathrm{d}f_{i}^{k}\;\right|\;n\in\mathbb{N},f_{i}^{j}\in{\rm Test}(M)\text{ for }0\leq j\leq k\right\},

\displaystyle{\rm Test}(\Lambda^{k}TM):

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}^{0}\nabla f_{i}^{1}\land\cdots\land\nabla f_{i}^{k}\;\right|\;n\in\mathbb{N},f_{i}^{j}\in{\rm Test}(M)\text{ for }0\leq j\leq k\right\},

\displaystyle{\rm Reg}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M):

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}^{0}\mathrm{d}f_{i}^{1}\land\cdots\land\mathrm{d}f_{i}^{k}\;\right|\;n\in\mathbb{N},f_{i}^{j}\in{\rm Test}(M)\text{ for }1\leq j\leq k,\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748ptf_{i}^{0}\in{\rm Test}(M)\cup\mathbb{R}{\bf 1}_{M}\Biggr\},

\displaystyle{\rm Reg}(\Lambda^{k}TM):

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.\sum_{i=1}^{n}f_{i}^{0}\mathrm{d}f_{i}^{1}\land\cdots\land\mathrm{d}f_{i}^{k}\;\right|\;n\in\mathbb{N},f_{i}^{j}\in{\rm Test}(M)\text{ for }1\leq j\leq k,\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748ptf_{i}^{0}\in{\rm Test}(M)\cup\mathbb{R}{\bf 1}_{M}\Biggr\}.

We employ the evident interpretations for
k=1
, while the respective spaces for
k=0
are identified with those spaces to which their generic elements’s zeroth order terms belong to. These classes are dense in their respective
L^{p}
-spaces,

p\in[1,+\infty]
, intended strongly if
p<\infty
and weakly* if
p=\infty
.
But the denseness in
L^{p}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)
(resp.
L^{p}(\Lambda^{k}TM)
) for
{\rm Reg}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)
(resp.
{\rm Reg}(\Lambda^{k}TM)
) should be modified as noted before.

5

Covariant derivative

5.1

The Sobolev spaces
W^{1,2}(TM)
and
H^{1,2}(TM)

We construct the tangent module
L^{2}(TM)
in Subsection
4.5
. Now we define the
(1,2)
-Sobolev space

W^{1,2}(TM)
based on the notion of
L^{2}
-Hessian.

Definition 5.1

(
(1,2)
-Sobolev space
W^{1,2}(TM)
)
.

The space
W^{1,2}(TM)
is defined to consist of all
X\in L^{2}(TM)
for which there exists a
T\in L^{2}(T^{\otimes 2}X)

such that for every
g_{1},g_{2},h\in{\rm Test}(M)
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}h\langle T\,|\,

\displaystyle\nabla g_{1}\otimes\nabla g_{2}\rangle\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}\langle X,\nabla g_{2}\rangle{\rm div}(h\nabla g_{1})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}-\int_{M}\,h\,{\rm Hess}\,g_{2}(X,\nabla g_{1})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}.

Here
{\rm Hess}\,g_{2}\in L^{2}((T^{*})^{\otimes 2}X)
is the
Hessian
defined for
g_{2}\in{\rm Test}(M)
(see
[
12
, Definition 5.2]
).
In case of existence, the element
T
is unique, denoted by
\nabla X
and called the
covariant derivative
of
X
.

Arguing as in
[
12
, after Definition 5.2]
, the uniqueness statement in Definition
5.1
is derived.
In particular,
W^{1,2}(TM)
constitutes a vector space and the covariant derivative
\nabla
is a linear operator on it.
The space
W^{1,2}(TM)
is endowed with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{W^{1,2}(M)}
given by

\displaystyle\|X\|_{W^{1,2}(TM)}^{2}:=\|X\|_{L^{2}(TM)}^{2}+\|\nabla X\|_{L^{2}(T^{\otimes 2}X)}^{2}.

We also define the
covariant functional

\mathscr{E}_{\rm cov}:L^{2}(TM)\to[0,+\infty[
by

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\rm cov}(X):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle{\int_{M}|\nabla X|_{\rm HS}^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}}&\text{ if }X\in W^{1,2}(TM),\\
\infty&\text{ otherwise. }\end{array}\right.

It is proved in
[
12
, Theorem 6.3]
that

(W^{1,2}(TM),\|\cdot\|_{W^{1,2}(TM)})
is a separable Hilbert space,
\nabla
is a closed operator,
{\rm Reg}(TM)\subset W^{1,2}(TM)
,
\|\cdot\|_{W^{1,2}(TM)}
-denseness of
W^{1,2}(TM)
in
L^{2}(TM)
, and the lower semi continuity of
\mathscr{E}_{\rm cov}:L^{2}(TM)\to[0,+\infty[
.

Definition 5.2

(
(1,2)
-Sobolev space
H^{1,2}(TM)
)
.

We define the space
H^{1,2}(TM)\subset W^{1,2}(TM)
as the
\|\cdot\|_{W^{12,}(TM)}
-closure of
{\rm Reg}(TM)
:

\displaystyle H^{1,2}(TM):=\overline{{\rm Reg}(TM)}^{\|\cdot\|_{W^{1,2}(TM)}}.

H^{1,2}(TM)
is in general a strict subset of
W^{1,2}(TM)
.

The following lemma is a version of what is known as
Kato’s inequality
(for the Bochner Laplacian)
in the smooth case (see
[
32
, Chapter 2]
,
[
31
, Lemma 3.5]
).

Lemma 5.3

(Kato’s inequality,
[
12
, Lemma 6.12]
)
.

For every
X\in H^{1,2}(TM)
,
|X|\in D(\mathscr{E})
and

\displaystyle|\nabla|X||\leq|\nabla X|_{\rm HS}\quad\mathfrak{m}\text{-a.e.}

In particular, if
X\in H^{1,2}(TM)\cap L^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{m})
, then
|X|^{2}\in D(\mathscr{E})
.

6

Exterior derivative

Throughout this section, we fix
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

6.1

The Sobolev space
D(\mathrm{d}^{k})
and
D(\mathrm{d}_{*}^{k})

Given
\omega\in L^{0}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)
and
X_{0},\cdots,X_{k},Y\in L^{0}(TM)
, we shall use the standard abbreviations: for
1\leq i<j\leq k

\displaystyle\omega(\widehat{X}_{i}):

\displaystyle=\omega(X_{0},\cdots,\widehat{X}_{i},\cdots,X_{k}),

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\omega(X_{0}\land\cdots\land X_{i-1}\land X_{i+1}\land\cdots\land X_{k}),

\displaystyle\omega(Y,\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{Y}_{j}):

\displaystyle=\omega(Y,X_{0},\cdots,\widehat{X}_{i},\cdots,\widehat{X}_{j},\cdots,X_{k}),

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\omega(Y\land X_{0}\land\cdots\land X_{i-1}\land X_{i+1}\land\cdots\land Y_{j-1}\land Y_{j+1}\land\cdots\land X_{k}).

Definition 6.1

(Sobolev space
D(\mathrm{d}^{k})
)
.

We define
D(\mathrm{d}^{k})
to consist of all
\omega\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)
for which there exists
\eta\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{k+1}T^{*}\!M)
such that for every
X_{0},\cdots,X_{k}\in{\rm Test}(M)
,

\displaystyle\int_{M}\eta(X_{0},\cdots,X_{k})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i+1}\omega(\widehat{X}_{i}){\rm div}\,X_{i}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\int_{M}\sum_{i=0}^{k}\sum_{j=i+1}^{k}(-1)^{i+j}\omega([X_{i},X_{j}],\widehat{X}_{i},\widehat{X}_{j})\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}.

In case of existence, the element
\eta
is unique, denoted by
\mathrm{d}\omega
and called the
exterior derivative

(or
exterior differential
) of
\omega
.

The uniqueness follows by density of
{\rm Test}(\Lambda^{k+1}T^{*}\!M)
in
L^{2}(\Lambda^{k+1}T^{*}\!M)
as discussed in
Section
4.8
. It is then clear that
D(\mathrm{d}^{k})
is a real vector space and that
\mathrm{d}
is
a linear operator on it.

We always endow
D(\mathrm{d}^{k})
with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{D(\mathrm{d}^{k})}
given by

\displaystyle\|\omega\|_{D(\mathrm{d}^{k})}^{2}:=\|\omega\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)}^{2}+\|\mathrm{d}\omega\|_{L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)}^{2}.

We introduce the functional
\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{d}}:L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)\to[0,+\infty]
with

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{d}}(\omega):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle{\int_{M}|\mathrm{d}\omega|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}}&\text{ if }\omega\in D(\mathrm{d}^{k})\\
\infty&\text{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

We do not make explicit the dependency of
\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{d}}
on the degree
k
. It will always be clear
from the context which one is intended. It is proved in
[
12
, Theorem 7.5]
that

(D(\mathrm{d}^{k}),\|\cdot\|_{D(\mathrm{d}^{k})})
is a separable Hilbert space, the exterior differential
\mathrm{d}
is a closed operator,

{\rm Reg}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)\subset D(\mathrm{d}^{k})
,
D(\mathrm{d}^{k})
is dense in
L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)
, and the functional

\mathscr{E}_{\mathrm{d}}:L^{2}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)\to[0,+\infty]
is lower semi continuous.

Definition 6.2

(The space
D_{\rm reg}(\mathrm{d}^{k})
)
.

We define the space
D_{\rm reg}(\mathrm{d}^{k})\subset D(\mathrm{d}^{k})
by the closure of
{\rm Reg}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)
with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{D(\mathrm{d}^{k})}
:

\displaystyle D_{\rm reg}(\mathrm{d}^{k}):=\overline{{\rm Reg}(\Lambda^{k}T^{*}\!M)}^{\|\cdot\|_{D(\mathrm{d}^{k})}}.

It is proved in
[
12
, Theorem 7.5]
that for every
\omega\in D_{\rm reg}(\mathrm{d}^{k})
, we have

\mathrm{d}\omega\in D_{\rm reg}(\mathrm{d}^{k+1})
with
\mathrm{d}(\mathrm{d}\omega)=0
.

Definition 6.3

(The space
D(\mathrm{d}_{*}^{k})
)
.

Given any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the space
D(\mathrm{d}_{*}^{k})
is defined to consist of all
